People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I’ve thought about it a few times, mostly because I’m embarrassed by how I came across. But I wouldn’t say it’s looping in my head or anything like that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on over and over?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’m not, you know, checking things over and over or feeling like I have to do some ritual, if that’s what you mean. I can be particular about work, but it doesn’t feel like an urge I can’t resist. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel are intrusive or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But yes, sometimes in the middle of a call, or when I’m just looking over numbers, that blast and the fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see. It’s not constant, but it is… unwelcome, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push those unwanted memories away when they come into your head?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… maybe more than I wanted to admit. I’ve been telling myself I’m just busy, but I’ve been keeping away from the new building. And there’s a mortgage folder and the safety reports sitting there unopened because when I go to pick them up, my stomach drops. So I leave them. That counts, doesn’t it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

My reaction on the board call made me realize this is starting to affect my judgment and leadership.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on over and over?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’m not, you know, checking things over and over or feeling like I have to do some ritual, if that’s what you mean. I can be particular about work, but it doesn’t feel like an urge I can’t resist. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel are intrusive or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But yes, sometimes in the middle of a call, or when I’m just looking over numbers, that blast and the fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see. It’s not constant, but it is… unwelcome, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push those unwanted memories away when they come into your head?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… maybe more than I wanted to admit. I’ve been telling myself I’m just busy, but I’ve been keeping away from the new building. And there’s a mortgage folder and the safety reports sitting there unopened because when I go to pick them up, my stomach drops. So I leave them. That counts, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the incident on the board call or the blast and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not in some dramatic way, but I have been keeping away from the new building. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or there are other people who can handle it, but… I know that’s not the whole truth.\n\nAnd there’s the mortgage folder and the safety reports, too. They’re just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I put them off. I guess that is avoiding it, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Has your behavior or these thoughts and feelings been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I’d like to say I’m still doing my job. I’m not falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I am delaying decisions about the facility, and I’ve been leaning on other people for calls I’d normally handle myself. That isn’t really like me. And after how I reacted on the board call, I had to admit it’s starting to affect my judgment a bit… maybe my leadership too. I don’t like saying that, but I suppose that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when these unwanted memories or thoughts come into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s upsetting, yes. I suppose that’s distress, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these unwanted memories and feelings of distress?\n\nPatient: About six days, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during the walkthrough.\n\nIt hasn’t been weeks or anything… just, well, almost a week now. I was hoping it would settle down on its own, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not everything, but I have been keeping away from the new building more than I probably should.\n\nAnd there are some papers—the mortgage folder, safety reports—that I keep meaning to open. They’re just sitting there. The moment I reach for them, my stomach sort of drops, and I find a reason to do something else. I know that probably isn’t ideal, but… yes, I suppose I am avoiding them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any time off work since the incident due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly time off, no. I mean, I’ve still been working… I’m not sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, I’ve been putting off some decisions about the facility, and I’ve asked other people to handle a few calls I would normally take myself. My leadership, too, which I don’t like saying. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to anyone in particular because of what happened?\n\nPatient: Not anyone in particular, no… at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been steering clear of things tied to it—the new building, mainly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I had a moment on a board call that didn’t sit right with me. Someone asked a pretty small question about the facility budget, and I snapped at them. I don’t usually handle myself that way—at least, I try not to. That’s not how I run my company or how I want to treat people, so… I thought I should talk to someone before it becomes anything more.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: I mean… not anxious in some obvious way, I don’t think. I’ve been under pressure, sure, but that’s not exactly new for me. Maybe I’m a little more on edge than I’d like to admit, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the incident on the board call?\n\nPatient: Not repeatedly, no… at least I don’t think so. I’ve thought about it a few times, mostly because I’m embarrassed by how I came across. But I wouldn’t say it’s looping in my head or anything like that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on over and over?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’m not, you know, checking things over and over or feeling like I have to do some ritual, if that’s what you mean. I can be particular about work, but it doesn’t feel like an urge I can’t resist. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel are intrusive or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But yes, sometimes in the middle of a call, or when I’m just looking over numbers, that blast and the fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see. It’s not constant, but it is… unwelcome, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push those unwanted memories away when they come into your head?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… maybe more than I wanted to admit. I’ve been telling myself I’m just busy, but I’ve been keeping away from the new building. And there’s a mortgage folder and the safety reports sitting there unopened because when I go to pick them up, my stomach drops. So I leave them. That counts, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the incident on the board call or the blast and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not in some dramatic way, but I have been keeping away from the new building. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or there are other people who can handle it, but… I know that’s not the whole truth.\n\nAnd there’s the mortgage folder and the safety reports, too. They’re just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I put them off. I guess that is avoiding it, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Has your behavior or these thoughts and feelings been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I’d like to say I’m still doing my job. I’m not falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I am delaying decisions about the facility, and I’ve been leaning on other people for calls I’d normally handle myself. That isn’t really like me. And after how I reacted on the board call, I had to admit it’s starting to affect my judgment a bit… maybe my leadership too. I don’t like saying that, but I suppose that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when these unwanted memories or thoughts come into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s upsetting, yes. I try not to make too much of it, because I know other people have dealt with worse, but when I’m in the middle of a call or just looking at numbers, the blast and that fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, but in that moment it throws me. My chest tightens a bit and I have to work to keep my voice normal. I suppose that’s distress, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these unwanted memories and feelings of distress?\n\nPatient: About six days, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during the walkthrough.\n\nIt hasn’t been weeks or anything… just, well, almost a week now. I was hoping it would settle down on its own, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not everything, but I have been keeping away from the new building more than I probably should.\n\nAnd there are some papers—the mortgage folder, safety reports—that I keep meaning to open. They’re just sitting there. The moment I reach for them, my stomach sort of drops, and I find a reason to do something else. I know that probably isn’t ideal, but… yes, I suppose I am avoiding them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any time off work since the incident due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly time off, no. I mean, I’ve still been working… I’m not sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, I’ve been putting off some decisions about the facility, and I’ve asked other people to handle a few calls I would normally take myself. I suppose I told myself that was just delegating, but after that board call—my reaction there—it made me think maybe this is affecting my judgment a bit. My leadership, too, which I don’t like saying. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to anyone in particular because of what happened?\n\nPatient: Not anyone in particular, no… at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been steering clear of things tied to it—the new building, mainly. And those documents, the mortgage folder and safety reports… I keep meaning to go through them, but when I reach for them my stomach drops, so I leave them sitting there. I know that’s not exactly the same as avoiding a person, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, sure, wondering if something could’ve been different. But I don’t think I feel guilty in the way you mean. At least… not that I’ve really noticed. Does that sound like a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities that were important to you before the incident?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like the things that usually settle me don’t quite do what they normally do. A chess problem, my hiking notes, even dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I know I should enjoy it, but it just feels flat. Like I can’t quite get to the feeling. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push those unwanted memories away when they come into your head?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… maybe more than I wanted to admit. I’ve been telling myself I’m just busy, but I’ve been keeping away from the new building. And there’s a mortgage folder and the safety reports sitting there unopened because when I go to pick them up, my stomach drops. So I leave them. That counts, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the incident on the board call or the blast and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not in some dramatic way, but I have been keeping away from the new building. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or there are other people who can handle it, but… I know that’s not the whole truth.\n\nAnd there’s the mortgage folder and the safety reports, too. They’re just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I put them off. I guess that is avoiding it, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Has your behavior or these thoughts and feelings been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I’d like to say I’m still doing my job. I’m not falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I am delaying decisions about the facility, and I’ve been leaning on other people for calls I’d normally handle myself. That isn’t really like me. And after how I reacted on the board call, I had to admit it’s starting to affect my judgment a bit… maybe my leadership too. I don’t like saying that, but I suppose that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when these unwanted memories or thoughts come into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s upsetting, yes. I try not to make too much of it, because I know other people have dealt with worse, but when I’m in the middle of a call or just looking at numbers, the blast and that fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, but in that moment it throws me. My chest tightens a bit and I have to work to keep my voice normal. I suppose that’s distress, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these unwanted memories and feelings of distress?\n\nPatient: About six days, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during the walkthrough.\n\nIt hasn’t been weeks or anything… just, well, almost a week now. I was hoping it would settle down on its own, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not everything, but I have been keeping away from the new building more than I probably should.\n\nAnd there are some papers—the mortgage folder, safety reports—that I keep meaning to open. They’re just sitting there. The moment I reach for them, my stomach sort of drops, and I find a reason to do something else. I know that probably isn’t ideal, but… yes, I suppose I am avoiding them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any time off work since the incident due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly time off, no. I mean, I’ve still been working… I’m not sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, I’ve been putting off some decisions about the facility, and I’ve asked other people to handle a few calls I would normally take myself. I suppose I told myself that was just delegating, but after that board call—my reaction there—it made me think maybe this is affecting my judgment a bit. My leadership, too, which I don’t like saying. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to anyone in particular because of what happened?\n\nPatient: Not anyone in particular, no… at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been steering clear of things tied to it—the new building, mainly. And those documents, the mortgage folder and safety reports… I keep meaning to go through them, but when I reach for them my stomach drops, so I leave them sitting there. I know that’s not exactly the same as avoiding a person, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, sure, wondering if something could’ve been different. But I don’t think I feel guilty in the way you mean. At least… not that I’ve really noticed. Does that sound like a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities that were important to you before the incident?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like the things that usually settle me don’t quite do what they normally do. A chess problem, my hiking notes, even dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I know I should enjoy it, but it just feels flat. Like I can’t quite get to the feeling. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to or being around certain people because of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Not certain people, no… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding anyone specifically.\n\nIt’s more the places and papers connected to it. I’ve been keeping away from the new building, and even the mortgage folder and those safety reports are still sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I just… leave them. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but that’s the avoidance I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

And after how I reacted on the board call, I had to admit it’s starting to affect my judgment a bit… maybe my leadership too. I don’t like saying that, but I suppose that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when these unwanted memories or thoughts come into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s upsetting, yes. I try not to make too much of it, because I know other people have dealt with worse, but when I’m in the middle of a call or just looking at numbers, the blast and that fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, but in that moment it throws me. My chest tightens a bit and I have to work to keep my voice normal. I suppose that’s distress, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these unwanted memories and feelings of distress?\n\nPatient: About six days, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during the walkthrough.\n\nIt hasn’t been weeks or anything… just, well, almost a week now. I was hoping it would settle down on its own, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not everything, but I have been keeping away from the new building more than I probably should.\n\nAnd there are some papers—the mortgage folder, safety reports—that I keep meaning to open. They’re just sitting there. The moment I reach for them, my stomach sort of drops, and I find a reason to do something else. I know that probably isn’t ideal, but… yes, I suppose I am avoiding them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any time off work since the incident due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly time off, no. I mean, I’ve still been working… I’m not sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, I’ve been putting off some decisions about the facility, and I’ve asked other people to handle a few calls I would normally take myself. I suppose I told myself that was just delegating, but after that board call—my reaction there—it made me think maybe this is affecting my judgment a bit. My leadership, too, which I don’t like saying. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to anyone in particular because of what happened?\n\nPatient: Not anyone in particular, no… at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been steering clear of things tied to it—the new building, mainly. And those documents, the mortgage folder and safety reports… I keep meaning to go through them, but when I reach for them my stomach drops, so I leave them sitting there. I know that’s not exactly the same as avoiding a person, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, sure, wondering if something could’ve been different. But I don’t think I feel guilty in the way you mean. At least… not that I’ve really noticed. Does that sound like a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities that were important to you before the incident?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like the things that usually settle me don’t quite do what they normally do. A chess problem, my hiking notes, even dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I know I should enjoy it, but it just feels flat. Like I can’t quite get to the feeling. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to or being around certain people because of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Not certain people, no… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding anyone specifically.\n\nIt’s more the places and papers connected to it. I’ve been keeping away from the new building, and even the mortgage folder and those safety reports are still sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I just… leave them. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but that’s the avoidance I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it trouble sleeping, exactly. I mean, maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve been keeping track of.\n\nSome nights I don’t fall off as easily as I used to, but I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m functioning. Is that… is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I guess that is avoiding it, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Has your behavior or these thoughts and feelings been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I’d like to say I’m still doing my job. I’m not falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I am delaying decisions about the facility, and I’ve been leaning on other people for calls I’d normally handle myself. That isn’t really like me. And after how I reacted on the board call, I had to admit it’s starting to affect my judgment a bit… maybe my leadership too. I don’t like saying that, but I suppose that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when these unwanted memories or thoughts come into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s upsetting, yes. I try not to make too much of it, because I know other people have dealt with worse, but when I’m in the middle of a call or just looking at numbers, the blast and that fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, but in that moment it throws me. My chest tightens a bit and I have to work to keep my voice normal. I suppose that’s distress, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these unwanted memories and feelings of distress?\n\nPatient: About six days, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during the walkthrough.\n\nIt hasn’t been weeks or anything… just, well, almost a week now. I was hoping it would settle down on its own, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not everything, but I have been keeping away from the new building more than I probably should.\n\nAnd there are some papers—the mortgage folder, safety reports—that I keep meaning to open. They’re just sitting there. The moment I reach for them, my stomach sort of drops, and I find a reason to do something else. I know that probably isn’t ideal, but… yes, I suppose I am avoiding them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any time off work since the incident due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly time off, no. I mean, I’ve still been working… I’m not sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, I’ve been putting off some decisions about the facility, and I’ve asked other people to handle a few calls I would normally take myself. I suppose I told myself that was just delegating, but after that board call—my reaction there—it made me think maybe this is affecting my judgment a bit. My leadership, too, which I don’t like saying. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to anyone in particular because of what happened?\n\nPatient: Not anyone in particular, no… at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been steering clear of things tied to it—the new building, mainly. And those documents, the mortgage folder and safety reports… I keep meaning to go through them, but when I reach for them my stomach drops, so I leave them sitting there. I know that’s not exactly the same as avoiding a person, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, sure, wondering if something could’ve been different. But I don’t think I feel guilty in the way you mean. At least… not that I’ve really noticed. Does that sound like a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities that were important to you before the incident?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like the things that usually settle me don’t quite do what they normally do. A chess problem, my hiking notes, even dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I know I should enjoy it, but it just feels flat. Like I can’t quite get to the feeling. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to or being around certain people because of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Not certain people, no… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding anyone specifically.\n\nIt’s more the places and papers connected to it. I’ve been keeping away from the new building, and even the mortgage folder and those safety reports are still sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I just… leave them. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but that’s the avoidance I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it trouble sleeping, exactly. I mean, maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve been keeping track of.\n\nSome nights I don’t fall off as easily as I used to, but I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m functioning. Is that… is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places besides the new building and those documents that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s much beyond that, honestly… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nThe main thing is still the new building, and anything tied to it on paper. The mortgage folder, the safety reports — I know I need to deal with them, but when I even start to reach for them, my stomach just drops, and I put them back. I suppose that counts as avoiding, doesn’t it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the incident on the board call?\n\nPatient: Not repeatedly, no… at least I don’t think so. I’ve thought about it a few times, mostly because I’m embarrassed by how I came across. But I wouldn’t say it’s looping in my head or anything like that. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on over and over?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’m not, you know, checking things over and over or feeling like I have to do some ritual, if that’s what you mean. I can be particular about work, but it doesn’t feel like an urge I can’t resist. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that you feel are intrusive or unwelcome?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. But yes, sometimes in the middle of a call, or when I’m just looking over numbers, that blast and the fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see. It’s not constant, but it is… unwelcome, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to push those unwanted memories away when they come into your head?\n\nPatient: I suppose I do, yes… maybe more than I wanted to admit. I’ve been telling myself I’m just busy, but I’ve been keeping away from the new building. And there’s a mortgage folder and the safety reports sitting there unopened because when I go to pick them up, my stomach drops. So I leave them. That counts, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the incident on the board call or the blast and fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not in some dramatic way, but I have been keeping away from the new building. I tell myself it’s just because I’m busy or there are other people who can handle it, but… I know that’s not the whole truth.\n\nAnd there’s the mortgage folder and the safety reports, too. They’re just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I put them off. I guess that is avoiding it, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Has your behavior or these thoughts and feelings been causing problems for you at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I’d like to say I’m still doing my job. I’m not falling apart or anything.\n\nBut I am delaying decisions about the facility, and I’ve been leaning on other people for calls I’d normally handle myself. That isn’t really like me. And after how I reacted on the board call, I had to admit it’s starting to affect my judgment a bit… maybe my leadership too. I don’t like saying that, but I suppose that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do you feel when these unwanted memories or thoughts come into your head?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s upsetting, yes. I try not to make too much of it, because I know other people have dealt with worse, but when I’m in the middle of a call or just looking at numbers, the blast and that fire by the loading area sort of jump back into my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nIt doesn’t last forever, but in that moment it throws me. My chest tightens a bit and I have to work to keep my voice normal. I suppose that’s distress, isn’t it?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these unwanted memories and feelings of distress?\n\nPatient: About six days, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during the walkthrough.\n\nIt hasn’t been weeks or anything… just, well, almost a week now. I was hoping it would settle down on its own, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… not everything, but I have been keeping away from the new building more than I probably should.\n\nAnd there are some papers—the mortgage folder, safety reports—that I keep meaning to open. They’re just sitting there. The moment I reach for them, my stomach sort of drops, and I find a reason to do something else. I know that probably isn’t ideal, but… yes, I suppose I am avoiding them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any time off work since the incident due to how you've been feeling?\n\nPatient: Not exactly time off, no. I mean, I’ve still been working… I’m not sitting at home or anything like that.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, I’ve been putting off some decisions about the facility, and I’ve asked other people to handle a few calls I would normally take myself. I suppose I told myself that was just delegating, but after that board call—my reaction there—it made me think maybe this is affecting my judgment a bit. My leadership, too, which I don’t like saying. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to anyone in particular because of what happened?\n\nPatient: Not anyone in particular, no… at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more that I’ve been steering clear of things tied to it—the new building, mainly. And those documents, the mortgage folder and safety reports… I keep meaning to go through them, but when I reach for them my stomach drops, so I leave them sitting there. I know that’s not exactly the same as avoiding a person, but that’s what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say that exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve gone over it in my head a few times, sure, wondering if something could’ve been different. But I don’t think I feel guilty in the way you mean. At least… not that I’ve really noticed. Does that sound like a reasonable answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in activities that were important to you before the incident?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more like the things that usually settle me don’t quite do what they normally do. A chess problem, my hiking notes, even dinner with my wife… I’m there, and I know I should enjoy it, but it just feels flat. Like I can’t quite get to the feeling. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to or being around certain people because of what happened during the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: Not certain people, no… I don’t think I’ve been avoiding anyone specifically.\n\nIt’s more the places and papers connected to it. I’ve been keeping away from the new building, and even the mortgage folder and those safety reports are still sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, so I just… leave them. I know that’s not exactly what you asked, but that’s the avoidance I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it trouble sleeping, exactly. I mean, maybe a little here and there, but nothing I’ve been keeping track of.\n\nSome nights I don’t fall off as easily as I used to, but I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m functioning. Is that… is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities, people, or places besides the new building and those documents that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the explosion and fire?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s much beyond that, honestly… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nThe main thing is still the new building, and anything tied to it on paper. The mortgage folder, the safety reports — I know I need to deal with them, but when I even start to reach for them, my stomach just drops, and I put them back. I suppose that counts as avoiding, doesn’t it?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms for more than a month now?\n\nPatient: No, not more than a month. It’s been… about six days now, since the explosion and fire during that walkthrough.\n\nSo, I mean, it feels longer in some ways, but it hasn’t actually been that long. Is that what you needed?"
